People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days are just kind of gray, but then the weather’s been gray too, and that apartment doesn’t get much sun after noon.

No, I’m not sure I’ve felt sad most days. I haven’t really kept track like that.

Nothing big comes to mind, anyway.\n\nI mean, there’s always little aggravations… the sink makes that knocking sound if you turn it too fast, and my mail gets mixed up with the lady downstairs sometimes. Last week I got a coupon flyer for dog food and I don’t even have a dog.\n\nBut around when I started feeling less… motivated? I don’t know. I don’t remember some big upset or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this lack of motivation?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s been a while. It sort of started after I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because I didn’t have to get up and be somewhere, you know, no bell schedules or papers to sort, no kids coming in needing passes… but then the days just kind of stretched out. So, yes, I’d say it’s been building since then.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you felt sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days are just kind of gray, but then the weather’s been gray too, and that apartment doesn’t get much sun after noon.\n\nI get tired of the same old things, I suppose. The news is always shouting, and the neighbor’s grandson parks too close to the curb so the delivery truck has trouble—well, that’s not what you asked.\n\nNo, I’m not sure I’ve felt sad most days. I haven’t really kept track like that.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Feeling bad about myself… hmm. I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I leave dishes sitting, or when I can’t remember where I put the little blue scissors—though those scissors never stay where they should, I swear.\n\nBut bad about myself, like… overall? I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Some days I just don’t get much done, and then the day’s gone, and the TV’s been on too long. But I don’t know if that’s feeling bad about myself exactly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two years, was there ever a stretch of at least two months when you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: Two months… back to my usual self? I don’t think so, no. It’s sort of been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago.\n\nThere might’ve been a good week here or there, like when I got some things sorted or the weather was nicer, but not a whole two months where I felt like how I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or low most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most days, exactly. Low is a funny word… like when the batteries are low in the remote, and then it only works if you hold it a certain way. I’ve had a lot of days where I’m just sort of… not up to much, but I don’t know if that’s “down.”\n\nSome days are all right if I keep busy with little things, though then I get sidetracked and the morning’s gone. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really counted the days like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I sit down to watch a program and then I realize I’m thinking about whether I left the laundry in, or that I need to wipe the counter, and then the program’s over. But that might just be normal, you know, getting older.\n\nI do lose my place sometimes if I’m reading the mail, especially those insurance papers with all the little print. My neighbor has these little flower pots on her steps, always crooked, and I think I ought to go say hello, but then I just… don’t.\n\nMost days I sit at home with the TV on low, not even really watching it, and I know people are expecting me places sometimes. I just can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, some days I feel kind of heavy, like when the house is too quiet… but then I’ll notice the dust on the lampshade, and I think about how my sister used to fuss over that kind of thing. She had this yellow duster, you know, the kind with the handle.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t been crying or anything like that. Just sort of… not much oomph, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory… well, I don’t know. I suppose I forget little things, like where I set my cup down, but doesn’t everybody? My aunt used to put her glasses right on top of her head and then holler for everyone to help her find them.\n\nI haven’t really noticed anything big, I don’t think. I mean… I keep track of my programs on TV when I bother to watch them, and I know which day the trash goes out, most of the time. So… I’m not sure what you mean by trouble, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or upsetting happen around the time this loss of motivation started?\n\nPatient: I can’t really put my finger on anything like that. Nothing big comes to mind, anyway.\n\nI mean, there’s always little aggravations… the sink makes that knocking sound if you turn it too fast, and my mail gets mixed up with the lady downstairs sometimes. Last week I got a coupon flyer for dog food and I don’t even have a dog.\n\nBut around when I started feeling less… motivated? I don’t know. I don’t remember some big upset or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this lack of motivation?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s been a while. It sort of started after I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because I didn’t have to get up and be somewhere, you know, no bell schedules or papers to sort, no kids coming in needing passes… but then the days just kind of stretched out. So, yes, I’d say it’s been building since then.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you felt sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days are just kind of gray, but then the weather’s been gray too, and that apartment doesn’t get much sun after noon.\n\nI get tired of the same old things, I suppose. The news is always shouting, and the neighbor’s grandson parks too close to the curb so the delivery truck has trouble—well, that’s not what you asked.\n\nNo, I’m not sure I’ve felt sad most days. I haven’t really kept track like that.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Feeling bad about myself… hmm. I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I leave dishes sitting, or when I can’t remember where I put the little blue scissors—though those scissors never stay where they should, I swear.\n\nBut bad about myself, like… overall? I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Some days I just don’t get much done, and then the day’s gone, and the TV’s been on too long. But I don’t know if that’s feeling bad about myself exactly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two years, was there ever a stretch of at least two months when you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: Two months… back to my usual self? I don’t think so, no. It’s sort of been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago.\n\nThere might’ve been a good week here or there, like when I got some things sorted or the weather was nicer, but not a whole two months where I felt like how I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or low most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most days, exactly. Low is a funny word… like when the batteries are low in the remote, and then it only works if you hold it a certain way. I’ve had a lot of days where I’m just sort of… not up to much, but I don’t know if that’s “down.”\n\nSome days are all right if I keep busy with little things, though then I get sidetracked and the morning’s gone. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really counted the days like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I sit down to watch a program and then I realize I’m thinking about whether I left the laundry in, or that I need to wipe the counter, and then the program’s over. But that might just be normal, you know, getting older.\n\nI do lose my place sometimes if I’m reading the mail, especially those insurance papers with all the little print. My neighbor says nobody can make sense of those anyway. So… trouble concentrating? Maybe a little here and there, but I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure I’d use that word. It sounds very final, doesn’t it? Like when a plant is too far gone and you throw it out, though sometimes if you put it by the kitchen window it comes back a little.\n\nI do get discouraged, I suppose. But “hopeless,” I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Low is a funny word… like when the batteries are low in the remote, and then it only works if you hold it a certain way. I’ve had a lot of days where I’m just sort of… not up to much, but I don’t know if that’s “down.”\n\nSome days are all right if I keep busy with little things, though then I get sidetracked and the morning’s gone. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really counted the days like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I sit down to watch a program and then I realize I’m thinking about whether I left the laundry in, or that I need to wipe the counter, and then the program’s over. But that might just be normal, you know, getting older.\n\nI do lose my place sometimes if I’m reading the mail, especially those insurance papers with all the little print. My neighbor says nobody can make sense of those anyway. So… trouble concentrating? Maybe a little here and there, but I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure I’d use that word. It sounds very final, doesn’t it? Like when a plant is too far gone and you throw it out, though sometimes if you put it by the kitchen window it comes back a little.\n\nI do get discouraged, I suppose. But “hopeless,” I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble managing your bills?\n\nPatient: The bills… well, I try to keep them in the little basket by the phone, but then the church bulletin gets mixed in, and those envelopes all look the same, don’t they?\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s only bills, exactly. I’ve had more trouble keeping up with things I said I’d do. I used to help lead the choir and do little projects at the senior center, and lately I had to step back some because I don’t always follow through like I promised. My church friends check on me more now, which is kind of them, but it makes me feel… I don’t know, a little embarrassed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I mean… I keep track of my programs on TV when I bother to watch them, and I know which day the trash goes out, most of the time. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to. Like with choir, and my quilting things, and going to visit folks—I used to do that more. My neighbor has these little flower pots on her steps, always crooked, and I think I ought to go say hello, but then I just… don’t.\n\nMost days I sit at home with the TV on low, not even really watching it, and I know people are expecting me places sometimes. I just can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, some days I feel kind of heavy, like when the house is too quiet… but then I’ll notice the dust on the lampshade, and I think about how my sister used to fuss over that kind of thing. She had this yellow duster, you know, the kind with the handle.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t been crying or anything like that. Just sort of… not much oomph, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory… well, I don’t know. I suppose I forget little things, like where I set my cup down, but doesn’t everybody? My aunt used to put her glasses right on top of her head and then holler for everyone to help her find them.\n\nI haven’t really noticed anything big, I don’t think. I mean… I keep track of my programs on TV when I bother to watch them, and I know which day the trash goes out, most of the time. So… I’m not sure what you mean by trouble, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or upsetting happen around the time this loss of motivation started?\n\nPatient: I can’t really put my finger on anything like that. Nothing big comes to mind, anyway.\n\nI mean, there’s always little aggravations… the sink makes that knocking sound if you turn it too fast, and my mail gets mixed up with the lady downstairs sometimes. Last week I got a coupon flyer for dog food and I don’t even have a dog.\n\nBut around when I started feeling less… motivated? I don’t know. I don’t remember some big upset or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this lack of motivation?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s been a while. It sort of started after I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because I didn’t have to get up and be somewhere, you know, no bell schedules or papers to sort, no kids coming in needing passes… but then the days just kind of stretched out. So, yes, I’d say it’s been building since then.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you felt sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days are just kind of gray, but then the weather’s been gray too, and that apartment doesn’t get much sun after noon.\n\nI get tired of the same old things, I suppose. The news is always shouting, and the neighbor’s grandson parks too close to the curb so the delivery truck has trouble—well, that’s not what you asked.\n\nNo, I’m not sure I’ve felt sad most days. I haven’t really kept track like that.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Feeling bad about myself… hmm. I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I leave dishes sitting, or when I can’t remember where I put the little blue scissors—though those scissors never stay where they should, I swear.\n\nBut bad about myself, like… overall? I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Some days I just don’t get much done, and then the day’s gone, and the TV’s been on too long. But I don’t know if that’s feeling bad about myself exactly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two years, was there ever a stretch of at least two months when you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: Two months… back to my usual self? I don’t think so, no. It’s sort of been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago.\n\nThere might’ve been a good week here or there, like when I got some things sorted or the weather was nicer, but not a whole two months where I felt like how I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or low most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most days, exactly. Low is a funny word… like when the batteries are low in the remote, and then it only works if you hold it a certain way. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really counted the days like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I sit down to watch a program and then I realize I’m thinking about whether I left the laundry in, or that I need to wipe the counter, and then the program’s over. But that might just be normal, you know, getting older.\n\nI do lose my place sometimes if I’m reading the mail, especially those insurance papers with all the little print. My neighbor says nobody can make sense of those anyway. So… trouble concentrating? Maybe a little here and there, but I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure I’d use that word. It sounds very final, doesn’t it? What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to. My neighbor has these little flower pots on her steps, always crooked, and I think I ought to go say hello, but then I just… don’t.\n\nMost days I sit at home with the TV on low, not even really watching it, and I know people are expecting me places sometimes. I just can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, some days I feel kind of heavy, like when the house is too quiet… but then I’ll notice the dust on the lampshade, and I think about how my sister used to fuss over that kind of thing. She had this yellow duster, you know, the kind with the handle.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t been crying or anything like that. Just sort of… not much oomph, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory… well, I don’t know. I suppose I forget little things, like where I set my cup down, but doesn’t everybody? My aunt used to put her glasses right on top of her head and then holler for everyone to help her find them.\n\nI haven’t really noticed anything big, I don’t think. I mean… I keep track of my programs on TV when I bother to watch them, and I know which day the trash goes out, most of the time. So… I’m not sure what you mean by trouble, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or upsetting happen around the time this loss of motivation started?\n\nPatient: I can’t really put my finger on anything like that. Nothing big comes to mind, anyway.\n\nI mean, there’s always little aggravations… the sink makes that knocking sound if you turn it too fast, and my mail gets mixed up with the lady downstairs sometimes. Last week I got a coupon flyer for dog food and I don’t even have a dog.\n\nBut around when I started feeling less… motivated? I don’t know. I don’t remember some big upset or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this lack of motivation?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s been a while. It sort of started after I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because I didn’t have to get up and be somewhere, you know, no bell schedules or papers to sort, no kids coming in needing passes… but then the days just kind of stretched out. So, yes, I’d say it’s been building since then.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you felt sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days are just kind of gray, but then the weather’s been gray too, and that apartment doesn’t get much sun after noon.\n\nI get tired of the same old things, I suppose. The news is always shouting, and the neighbor’s grandson parks too close to the curb so the delivery truck has trouble—well, that’s not what you asked.\n\nNo, I’m not sure I’ve felt sad most days. I haven’t really kept track like that.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Feeling bad about myself… hmm. I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I leave dishes sitting, or when I can’t remember where I put the little blue scissors—though those scissors never stay where they should, I swear.\n\nBut bad about myself, like… overall? I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Some days I just don’t get much done, and then the day’s gone, and the TV’s been on too long. But I don’t know if that’s feeling bad about myself exactly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two years, was there ever a stretch of at least two months when you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: Two months… back to my usual self? I don’t think so, no. It’s sort of been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago.\n\nThere might’ve been a good week here or there, like when I got some things sorted or the weather was nicer, but not a whole two months where I felt like how I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or low most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most days, exactly. Low is a funny word… like when the batteries are low in the remote, and then it only works if you hold it a certain way. I’ve had a lot of days where I’m just sort of… not up to much, but I don’t know if that’s “down.”\n\nSome days are all right if I keep busy with little things, though then I get sidetracked and the morning’s gone. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really counted the days like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I sit down to watch a program and then I realize I’m thinking about whether I left the laundry in, or that I need to wipe the counter, and then the program’s over. But that might just be normal, you know, getting older.\n\nI do lose my place sometimes if I’m reading the mail, especially those insurance papers with all the little print. My neighbor says nobody can make sense of those anyway. So… trouble concentrating? Maybe a little here and there, but I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure I’d use that word. It sounds very final, doesn’t it? Like when a plant is too far gone and you throw it out, though sometimes if you put it by the kitchen window it comes back a little.\n\nI do get discouraged, I suppose. But “hopeless,” I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble managing your bills?\n\nPatient: The bills… well, I try to keep them in the little basket by the phone, but then the church bulletin gets mixed in, and those envelopes all look the same, don’t they?\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s only bills, exactly.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF34.1, F32.9, F33.9, F06.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to. Like with choir, and my quilting things, and going to visit folks—I used to do that more. My neighbor has these little flower pots on her steps, always crooked, and I think I ought to go say hello, but then I just… don’t.\n\nMost days I sit at home with the TV on low, not even really watching it, and I know people are expecting me places sometimes. I just can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, some days I feel kind of heavy, like when the house is too quiet… but then I’ll notice the dust on the lampshade, and I think about how my sister used to fuss over that kind of thing. She had this yellow duster, you know, the kind with the handle.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t been crying or anything like that. Just sort of… not much oomph, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory… well, I don’t know. I suppose I forget little things, like where I set my cup down, but doesn’t everybody? My aunt used to put her glasses right on top of her head and then holler for everyone to help her find them.\n\nI haven’t really noticed anything big, I don’t think. I mean… I keep track of my programs on TV when I bother to watch them, and I know which day the trash goes out, most of the time. So… I’m not sure what you mean by trouble, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or upsetting happen around the time this loss of motivation started?\n\nPatient: I can’t really put my finger on anything like that. Nothing big comes to mind, anyway.\n\nI mean, there’s always little aggravations… the sink makes that knocking sound if you turn it too fast, and my mail gets mixed up with the lady downstairs sometimes. Last week I got a coupon flyer for dog food and I don’t even have a dog.\n\nBut around when I started feeling less… motivated? I don’t know. I don’t remember some big upset or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this lack of motivation?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s been a while. It sort of started after I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because I didn’t have to get up and be somewhere, you know, no bell schedules or papers to sort, no kids coming in needing passes… but then the days just kind of stretched out. So, yes, I’d say it’s been building since then.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you felt sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days are just kind of gray, but then the weather’s been gray too, and that apartment doesn’t get much sun after noon.\n\nI get tired of the same old things, I suppose. The news is always shouting, and the neighbor’s grandson parks too close to the curb so the delivery truck has trouble—well, that’s not what you asked.\n\nNo, I’m not sure I’ve felt sad most days. I haven’t really kept track like that.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Feeling bad about myself… hmm. I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I leave dishes sitting, or when I can’t remember where I put the little blue scissors—though those scissors never stay where they should, I swear.\n\nBut bad about myself, like… overall? I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Some days I just don’t get much done, and then the day’s gone, and the TV’s been on too long. But I don’t know if that’s feeling bad about myself exactly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two years, was there ever a stretch of at least two months when you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: Two months… back to my usual self? I don’t think so, no. It’s sort of been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago.\n\nThere might’ve been a good week here or there, like when I got some things sorted or the weather was nicer, but not a whole two months where I felt like how I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or low most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most days, exactly. Low is a funny word… like when the batteries are low in the remote, and then it only works if you hold it a certain way. I’ve had a lot of days where I’m just sort of… not up to much, but I don’t know if that’s “down.”\n\nSome days are all right if I keep busy with little things, though then I get sidetracked and the morning’s gone. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really counted the days like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I sit down to watch a program and then I realize I’m thinking about whether I left the laundry in, or that I need to wipe the counter, and then the program’s over. But that might just be normal, you know, getting older.\n\nI do lose my place sometimes if I’m reading the mail, especially those insurance papers with all the little print. My neighbor says nobody can make sense of those anyway. So… trouble concentrating? Maybe a little here and there, but I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure I’d use that word. It sounds very final, doesn’t it? Like when a plant is too far gone and you throw it out, though sometimes if you put it by the kitchen window it comes back a little.\n\nI do get discouraged, I suppose. But “hopeless,” I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble managing your bills?\n\nPatient: The bills… well, I try to keep them in the little basket by the phone, but then the church bulletin gets mixed in, and those envelopes all look the same, don’t they?\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s only bills, exactly. I’ve had more trouble keeping up with things I said I’d do. I used to help lead the choir and do little projects at the senior center, and lately I had to step back some because I don’t always follow through like I promised. My church friends check on me more now, which is kind of them, but it makes me feel… I don’t know, a little embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you they’ve noticed your memory or thinking has gotten worse?\n\nPatient: Other people… I don’t know if they’ve said it like that, exactly. My neighbor will sometimes say, “Now don’t forget,” but she says that to everybody, even when she’s reminding herself about the grocery coupons.\n\nMy church friends notice if I don’t bring something I said I would, but that could be because I put it by the door and then see a dish in the sink and—well, one thing leads to another. I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said my thinking is worse. Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: For the past two years, have you felt depressed most of the day on more days than not?\n\nPatient: For two years… my goodness, that’s a long stretch to look back on. I don’t keep track of days like that, you know. Some days feel heavy, sure, especially when the weather sits gray and the house is too quiet, but then other days I’m all right enough, or I’m thinking about something else entirely.\n\nI wouldn’t know how to say “most of the day, more days than not.” That sounds like counting beans in a jar. I just… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory or thinking seems worse than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Close to me… well, my sister will say, “You already told me that,” but she’s always been quick with me, even when we were girls sharing a room and she’d fuss over which drawer was hers.\n\nI don’t know that anyone has really told me my thinking is worse. They might remind me of things, church people especially, but nobody’s come right out and said it like that. Or if they did, maybe I didn’t take it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with your medications?\n\nPatient: Help with them… I’m not sure what you mean exactly. Like someone handing them to me, or just reminding me? I keep them where I can see them, mostly, though sometimes things get moved when I’m cleaning, and then I’ll find old mail I meant to throw out, and that turns into a whole morning.\n\nI don’t know that I’d say I need help, but… reminders don’t hurt, I suppose.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

My money… well, I still try to keep hold of it myself, you know. I’ve always been careful, my mother used to say a dollar had corners so it wouldn’t roll away.

But I have stepped back from some things I used to keep up with better—like leading choir, and helping with those little senior-center projects—because I don’t always remember what I promised people I’d do. Then folks from church, they check on me more than they used to. Not exactly managing my money, maybe, but they do look in on me.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF34.1, F32.9, F33.9, F06.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to. Like with choir, and my quilting things, and going to visit folks—I used to do that more. My neighbor has these little flower pots on her steps, always crooked, and I think I ought to go say hello, but then I just… don’t.\n\nMost days I sit at home with the TV on low, not even really watching it, and I know people are expecting me places sometimes. I just can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, some days I feel kind of heavy, like when the house is too quiet… but then I’ll notice the dust on the lampshade, and I think about how my sister used to fuss over that kind of thing. She had this yellow duster, you know, the kind with the handle.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t been crying or anything like that. Just sort of… not much oomph, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory… well, I don’t know. I suppose I forget little things, like where I set my cup down, but doesn’t everybody? My aunt used to put her glasses right on top of her head and then holler for everyone to help her find them.\n\nI haven’t really noticed anything big, I don’t think. I mean… I keep track of my programs on TV when I bother to watch them, and I know which day the trash goes out, most of the time. So… I’m not sure what you mean by trouble, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or upsetting happen around the time this loss of motivation started?\n\nPatient: I can’t really put my finger on anything like that. Nothing big comes to mind, anyway.\n\nI mean, there’s always little aggravations… the sink makes that knocking sound if you turn it too fast, and my mail gets mixed up with the lady downstairs sometimes. Last week I got a coupon flyer for dog food and I don’t even have a dog.\n\nBut around when I started feeling less… motivated? I don’t know. I don’t remember some big upset or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this lack of motivation?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s been a while. It sort of started after I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because I didn’t have to get up and be somewhere, you know, no bell schedules or papers to sort, no kids coming in needing passes… but then the days just kind of stretched out. So, yes, I’d say it’s been building since then.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you felt sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days are just kind of gray, but then the weather’s been gray too, and that apartment doesn’t get much sun after noon.\n\nI get tired of the same old things, I suppose. The news is always shouting, and the neighbor’s grandson parks too close to the curb so the delivery truck has trouble—well, that’s not what you asked.\n\nNo, I’m not sure I’ve felt sad most days. I haven’t really kept track like that.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Feeling bad about myself… hmm. I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I leave dishes sitting, or when I can’t remember where I put the little blue scissors—though those scissors never stay where they should, I swear.\n\nBut bad about myself, like… overall? I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Some days I just don’t get much done, and then the day’s gone, and the TV’s been on too long. But I don’t know if that’s feeling bad about myself exactly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two years, was there ever a stretch of at least two months when you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: Two months… back to my usual self? I don’t think so, no. It’s sort of been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago.\n\nThere might’ve been a good week here or there, like when I got some things sorted or the weather was nicer, but not a whole two months where I felt like how I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or low most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most days, exactly. Low is a funny word… like when the batteries are low in the remote, and then it only works if you hold it a certain way. I’ve had a lot of days where I’m just sort of… not up to much, but I don’t know if that’s “down.”\n\nSome days are all right if I keep busy with little things, though then I get sidetracked and the morning’s gone. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really counted the days like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I sit down to watch a program and then I realize I’m thinking about whether I left the laundry in, or that I need to wipe the counter, and then the program’s over. But that might just be normal, you know, getting older.\n\nI do lose my place sometimes if I’m reading the mail, especially those insurance papers with all the little print. My neighbor says nobody can make sense of those anyway. So… trouble concentrating? Maybe a little here and there, but I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure I’d use that word. It sounds very final, doesn’t it? Like when a plant is too far gone and you throw it out, though sometimes if you put it by the kitchen window it comes back a little.\n\nI do get discouraged, I suppose. But “hopeless,” I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble managing your bills?\n\nPatient: The bills… well, I try to keep them in the little basket by the phone, but then the church bulletin gets mixed in, and those envelopes all look the same, don’t they?\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s only bills, exactly. I’ve had more trouble keeping up with things I said I’d do. I used to help lead the choir and do little projects at the senior center, and lately I had to step back some because I don’t always follow through like I promised. My church friends check on me more now, which is kind of them, but it makes me feel… I don’t know, a little embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you they’ve noticed your memory or thinking has gotten worse?\n\nPatient: Other people… I don’t know if they’ve said it like that, exactly. My neighbor will sometimes say, “Now don’t forget,” but she says that to everybody, even when she’s reminding herself about the grocery coupons.\n\nMy church friends notice if I don’t bring something I said I would, but that could be because I put it by the door and then see a dish in the sink and—well, one thing leads to another. I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said my thinking is worse. Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: For the past two years, have you felt depressed most of the day on more days than not?\n\nPatient: For two years… my goodness, that’s a long stretch to look back on. I don’t keep track of days like that, you know. Some days feel heavy, sure, especially when the weather sits gray and the house is too quiet, but then other days I’m all right enough, or I’m thinking about something else entirely.\n\nI wouldn’t know how to say “most of the day, more days than not.” That sounds like counting beans in a jar. I just… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory or thinking seems worse than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Close to me… well, my sister will say, “You already told me that,” but she’s always been quick with me, even when we were girls sharing a room and she’d fuss over which drawer was hers.\n\nI don’t know that anyone has really told me my thinking is worse. They might remind me of things, church people especially, but nobody’s come right out and said it like that. Or if they did, maybe I didn’t take it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with your medications?\n\nPatient: Help with them… I’m not sure what you mean exactly. Like someone handing them to me, or just reminding me? I keep them where I can see them, mostly, though sometimes things get moved when I’m cleaning, and then I’ll find old mail I meant to throw out, and that turns into a whole morning.\n\nI don’t know that I’d say I need help, but… reminders don’t hurt, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you now need help managing your money?\n\nPatient: My money… well, I still try to keep hold of it myself, you know. I’ve always been careful, my mother used to say a dollar had corners so it wouldn’t roll away.\n\nBut I have stepped back from some things I used to keep up with better—like leading choir, and helping with those little senior-center projects—because I don’t always remember what I promised people I’d do. Then folks from church, they check on me more than they used to. Not exactly managing my money, maybe, but they do look in on me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’ve always been careful, my mother used to say a dollar had corners so it wouldn’t roll away.\n\nBut I have stepped back from some things I used to keep up with better—like leading choir, and helping with those little senior-center projects—because I don’t always remember what I promised people I’d do.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF34.1, F32.9, F33.9, F06.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to. Like with choir, and my quilting things, and going to visit folks—I used to do that more. My neighbor has these little flower pots on her steps, always crooked, and I think I ought to go say hello, but then I just… don’t.\n\nMost days I sit at home with the TV on low, not even really watching it, and I know people are expecting me places sometimes. I just can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, some days I feel kind of heavy, like when the house is too quiet… but then I’ll notice the dust on the lampshade, and I think about how my sister used to fuss over that kind of thing. She had this yellow duster, you know, the kind with the handle.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t been crying or anything like that. Just sort of… not much oomph, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory… well, I don’t know. I suppose I forget little things, like where I set my cup down, but doesn’t everybody? My aunt used to put her glasses right on top of her head and then holler for everyone to help her find them.\n\nI haven’t really noticed anything big, I don’t think. I mean… I keep track of my programs on TV when I bother to watch them, and I know which day the trash goes out, most of the time. So… I’m not sure what you mean by trouble, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or upsetting happen around the time this loss of motivation started?\n\nPatient: I can’t really put my finger on anything like that. Nothing big comes to mind, anyway.\n\nI mean, there’s always little aggravations… the sink makes that knocking sound if you turn it too fast, and my mail gets mixed up with the lady downstairs sometimes. Last week I got a coupon flyer for dog food and I don’t even have a dog.\n\nBut around when I started feeling less… motivated? I don’t know. I don’t remember some big upset or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this lack of motivation?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s been a while. It sort of started after I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because I didn’t have to get up and be somewhere, you know, no bell schedules or papers to sort, no kids coming in needing passes… but then the days just kind of stretched out. So, yes, I’d say it’s been building since then.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you felt sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days are just kind of gray, but then the weather’s been gray too, and that apartment doesn’t get much sun after noon.\n\nI get tired of the same old things, I suppose. The news is always shouting, and the neighbor’s grandson parks too close to the curb so the delivery truck has trouble—well, that’s not what you asked.\n\nNo, I’m not sure I’ve felt sad most days. I haven’t really kept track like that.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Feeling bad about myself… hmm. I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I leave dishes sitting, or when I can’t remember where I put the little blue scissors—though those scissors never stay where they should, I swear.\n\nBut bad about myself, like… overall? I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Some days I just don’t get much done, and then the day’s gone, and the TV’s been on too long. But I don’t know if that’s feeling bad about myself exactly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two years, was there ever a stretch of at least two months when you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: Two months… back to my usual self? I don’t think so, no. It’s sort of been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago.\n\nThere might’ve been a good week here or there, like when I got some things sorted or the weather was nicer, but not a whole two months where I felt like how I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or low most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most days, exactly. Low is a funny word… like when the batteries are low in the remote, and then it only works if you hold it a certain way. I’ve had a lot of days where I’m just sort of… not up to much, but I don’t know if that’s “down.”\n\nSome days are all right if I keep busy with little things, though then I get sidetracked and the morning’s gone. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really counted the days like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I sit down to watch a program and then I realize I’m thinking about whether I left the laundry in, or that I need to wipe the counter, and then the program’s over. But that might just be normal, you know, getting older.\n\nI do lose my place sometimes if I’m reading the mail, especially those insurance papers with all the little print. My neighbor says nobody can make sense of those anyway. So… trouble concentrating? Maybe a little here and there, but I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure I’d use that word. It sounds very final, doesn’t it? Like when a plant is too far gone and you throw it out, though sometimes if you put it by the kitchen window it comes back a little.\n\nI do get discouraged, I suppose. But “hopeless,” I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble managing your bills?\n\nPatient: The bills… well, I try to keep them in the little basket by the phone, but then the church bulletin gets mixed in, and those envelopes all look the same, don’t they?\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s only bills, exactly. I’ve had more trouble keeping up with things I said I’d do. I used to help lead the choir and do little projects at the senior center, and lately I had to step back some because I don’t always follow through like I promised. My church friends check on me more now, which is kind of them, but it makes me feel… I don’t know, a little embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you they’ve noticed your memory or thinking has gotten worse?\n\nPatient: Other people… I don’t know if they’ve said it like that, exactly. My neighbor will sometimes say, “Now don’t forget,” but she says that to everybody, even when she’s reminding herself about the grocery coupons.\n\nMy church friends notice if I don’t bring something I said I would, but that could be because I put it by the door and then see a dish in the sink and—well, one thing leads to another. I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said my thinking is worse. Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: For the past two years, have you felt depressed most of the day on more days than not?\n\nPatient: For two years… my goodness, that’s a long stretch to look back on. I don’t keep track of days like that, you know. Some days feel heavy, sure, especially when the weather sits gray and the house is too quiet, but then other days I’m all right enough, or I’m thinking about something else entirely.\n\nI wouldn’t know how to say “most of the day, more days than not.” That sounds like counting beans in a jar. I just… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory or thinking seems worse than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Close to me… well, my sister will say, “You already told me that,” but she’s always been quick with me, even when we were girls sharing a room and she’d fuss over which drawer was hers.\n\nI don’t know that anyone has really told me my thinking is worse. They might remind me of things, church people especially, but nobody’s come right out and said it like that. Or if they did, maybe I didn’t take it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with your medications?\n\nPatient: Help with them… I’m not sure what you mean exactly. Like someone handing them to me, or just reminding me? I keep them where I can see them, mostly, though sometimes things get moved when I’m cleaning, and then I’ll find old mail I meant to throw out, and that turns into a whole morning.\n\nI don’t know that I’d say I need help, but… reminders don’t hurt, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you now need help managing your money?\n\nPatient: My money… well, I still try to keep hold of it myself, you know. I’ve always been careful, my mother used to say a dollar had corners so it wouldn’t roll away.\n\nBut I have stepped back from some things I used to keep up with better—like leading choir, and helping with those little senior-center projects—because I don’t always remember what I promised people I’d do. Then folks from church, they check on me more than they used to. Not exactly managing my money, maybe, but they do look in on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help keeping track of appointments?\n\nPatient: Appointments… I try to write them down when they give me the little card, though those cards are so small, and I’ll put one in my purse and then later I’m looking for a receipt from the grocery and there it is.\n\nI don’t know if I need help exactly. Sometimes someone will remind me, but I still try to keep track. It depends what you mean by help, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

My church friends check on me more now, which is kind of them, but it makes me feel… I don’t know, a little embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you they’ve noticed your memory or thinking has gotten worse?\n\nPatient: Other people… I don’t know if they’ve said it like that, exactly. My neighbor will sometimes say, “Now don’t forget,” but she says that to everybody, even when she’s reminding herself about the grocery coupons.\n\nMy church friends notice if I don’t bring something I said I would, but that could be because I put it by the door and then see a dish in the sink and—well, one thing leads to another. I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said my thinking is worse. Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: For the past two years, have you felt depressed most of the day on more days than not?\n\nPatient: For two years… my goodness, that’s a long stretch to look back on. I don’t keep track of days like that, you know. Some days feel heavy, sure, especially when the weather sits gray and the house is too quiet, but then other days I’m all right enough, or I’m thinking about something else entirely.\n\nI wouldn’t know how to say “most of the day, more days than not.” That sounds like counting beans in a jar. I just… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory or thinking seems worse than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Close to me… well, my sister will say, “You already told me that,” but she’s always been quick with me, even when we were girls sharing a room and she’d fuss over which drawer was hers.\n\nI don’t know that anyone has really told me my thinking is worse. They might remind me of things, church people especially, but nobody’s come right out and said it like that. Or if they did, maybe I didn’t take it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with your medications?\n\nPatient: Help with them… I’m not sure what you mean exactly. Like someone handing them to me, or just reminding me? I keep them where I can see them, mostly, though sometimes things get moved when I’m cleaning, and then I’ll find old mail I meant to throw out, and that turns into a whole morning.\n\nI don’t know that I’d say I need help, but… reminders don’t hurt, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you now need help managing your money?\n\nPatient: My money… well, I still try to keep hold of it myself, you know. I’ve always been careful, my mother used to say a dollar had corners so it wouldn’t roll away.\n\nBut I have stepped back from some things I used to keep up with better—like leading choir, and helping with those little senior-center projects—because I don’t always remember what I promised people I’d do. Then folks from church, they check on me more than they used to. Not exactly managing my money, maybe, but they do look in on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help keeping track of appointments?\n\nPatient: Appointments… I try to write them down when they give me the little card, though those cards are so small, and I’ll put one in my purse and then later I’m looking for a receipt from the grocery and there it is.\n\nI don’t know if I need help exactly. Sometimes someone will remind me, but I still try to keep track. It depends what you mean by help, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to. I just can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, some days I feel kind of heavy, like when the house is too quiet… but then I’ll notice the dust on the lampshade, and I think about how my sister used to fuss over that kind of thing. She had this yellow duster, you know, the kind with the handle.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t been crying or anything like that. Just sort of… not much oomph, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory… well, I don’t know. I suppose I forget little things, like where I set my cup down, but doesn’t everybody? My aunt used to put her glasses right on top of her head and then holler for everyone to help her find them.\n\nI haven’t really noticed anything big, I don’t think. I mean… I keep track of my programs on TV when I bother to watch them, and I know which day the trash goes out, most of the time. So… I’m not sure what you mean by trouble, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or upsetting happen around the time this loss of motivation started?\n\nPatient: I can’t really put my finger on anything like that. Nothing big comes to mind, anyway.\n\nI mean, there’s always little aggravations… the sink makes that knocking sound if you turn it too fast, and my mail gets mixed up with the lady downstairs sometimes. Last week I got a coupon flyer for dog food and I don’t even have a dog.\n\nBut around when I started feeling less… motivated? I don’t know. I don’t remember some big upset or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this lack of motivation?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s been a while. It sort of started after I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because I didn’t have to get up and be somewhere, you know, no bell schedules or papers to sort, no kids coming in needing passes… but then the days just kind of stretched out. So, yes, I’d say it’s been building since then.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you felt sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Some days are just kind of gray, but then the weather’s been gray too, and that apartment doesn’t get much sun after noon.\n\nI get tired of the same old things, I suppose. The news is always shouting, and the neighbor’s grandson parks too close to the curb so the delivery truck has trouble—well, that’s not what you asked.\n\nNo, I’m not sure I’ve felt sad most days. I haven’t really kept track like that.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Feeling bad about myself… hmm. I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I leave dishes sitting, or when I can’t remember where I put the little blue scissors—though those scissors never stay where they should, I swear.\n\nBut bad about myself, like… overall? I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Some days I just don’t get much done, and then the day’s gone, and the TV’s been on too long. But I don’t know if that’s feeling bad about myself exactly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two years, was there ever a stretch of at least two months when you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: Two months… back to my usual self? I don’t think so, no. It’s sort of been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago.\n\nThere might’ve been a good week here or there, like when I got some things sorted or the weather was nicer, but not a whole two months where I felt like how I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or low most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most days, exactly. Low is a funny word… like when the batteries are low in the remote, and then it only works if you hold it a certain way. I’ve had a lot of days where I’m just sort of… not up to much, but I don’t know if that’s “down.”\n\nSome days are all right if I keep busy with little things, though then I get sidetracked and the morning’s gone. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really counted the days like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I sit down to watch a program and then I realize I’m thinking about whether I left the laundry in, or that I need to wipe the counter, and then the program’s over. But that might just be normal, you know, getting older.\n\nI do lose my place sometimes if I’m reading the mail, especially those insurance papers with all the little print. My neighbor says nobody can make sense of those anyway. So… trouble concentrating? Maybe a little here and there, but I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure I’d use that word. It sounds very final, doesn’t it? Like when a plant is too far gone and you throw it out, though sometimes if you put it by the kitchen window it comes back a little.\n\nI do get discouraged, I suppose. But “hopeless,” I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble managing your bills?\n\nPatient: The bills… well, I try to keep them in the little basket by the phone, but then the church bulletin gets mixed in, and those envelopes all look the same, don’t they?\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s only bills, exactly. I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Some days I just don’t get much done, and then the day’s gone, and the TV’s been on too long. But I don’t know if that’s feeling bad about myself exactly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two years, was there ever a stretch of at least two months when you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: Two months… back to my usual self? I don’t think so, no. It’s sort of been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago.\n\nThere might’ve been a good week here or there, like when I got some things sorted or the weather was nicer, but not a whole two months where I felt like how I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or low most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most days, exactly. Low is a funny word… like when the batteries are low in the remote, and then it only works if you hold it a certain way. I’ve had a lot of days where I’m just sort of… not up to much, but I don’t know if that’s “down.”\n\nSome days are all right if I keep busy with little things, though then I get sidetracked and the morning’s gone. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really counted the days like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I sit down to watch a program and then I realize I’m thinking about whether I left the laundry in, or that I need to wipe the counter, and then the program’s over. But that might just be normal, you know, getting older.\n\nI do lose my place sometimes if I’m reading the mail, especially those insurance papers with all the little print. My neighbor says nobody can make sense of those anyway. So… trouble concentrating? Maybe a little here and there, but I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure I’d use that word. It sounds very final, doesn’t it? Like when a plant is too far gone and you throw it out, though sometimes if you put it by the kitchen window it comes back a little.\n\nI do get discouraged, I suppose. But “hopeless,” I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble managing your bills?\n\nPatient: The bills… well, I try to keep them in the little basket by the phone, but then the church bulletin gets mixed in, and those envelopes all look the same, don’t they?\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s only bills, exactly. I’ve had more trouble keeping up with things I said I’d do. I used to help lead the choir and do little projects at the senior center, and lately I had to step back some because I don’t always follow through like I promised. My church friends check on me more now, which is kind of them, but it makes me feel… I don’t know, a little embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you they’ve noticed your memory or thinking has gotten worse?\n\nPatient: Other people… I don’t know if they’ve said it like that, exactly. My neighbor will sometimes say, “Now don’t forget,” but she says that to everybody, even when she’s reminding herself about the grocery coupons.\n\nMy church friends notice if I don’t bring something I said I would, but that could be because I put it by the door and then see a dish in the sink and—well, one thing leads to another. I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said my thinking is worse. Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: For the past two years, have you felt depressed most of the day on more days than not?\n\nPatient: For two years… my goodness, that’s a long stretch to look back on. I don’t keep track of days like that, you know. Some days feel heavy, sure, especially when the weather sits gray and the house is too quiet, but then other days I’m all right enough, or I’m thinking about something else entirely.\n\nI wouldn’t know how to say “most of the day, more days than not.” That sounds like counting beans in a jar. I just… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory or thinking seems worse than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Close to me… well, my sister will say, “You already told me that,” but she’s always been quick with me, even when we were girls sharing a room and she’d fuss over which drawer was hers.\n\nI don’t know that anyone has really told me my thinking is worse. They might remind me of things, church people especially, but nobody’s come right out and said it like that. Or if they did, maybe I didn’t take it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with your medications?\n\nPatient: Help with them… I’m not sure what you mean exactly. Like someone handing them to me, or just reminding me? I keep them where I can see them, mostly, though sometimes things get moved when I’m cleaning, and then I’ll find old mail I meant to throw out, and that turns into a whole morning.\n\nI don’t know that I’d say I need help, but… reminders don’t hurt, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you now need help managing your money?\n\nPatient: My money… well, I still try to keep hold of it myself, you know. I’ve always been careful, my mother used to say a dollar had corners so it wouldn’t roll away.\n\nBut I have stepped back from some things I used to keep up with better—like leading choir, and helping with those little senior-center projects—because I don’t always remember what I promised people I’d do. Then folks from church, they check on me more than they used to. Not exactly managing my money, maybe, but they do look in on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help keeping track of appointments?\n\nPatient: Appointments… I try to write them down when they give me the little card, though those cards are so small, and I’ll put one in my purse and then later I’m looking for a receipt from the grocery and there it is.\n\nI don’t know if I need help exactly. Sometimes someone will remind me, but I still try to keep track. It depends what you mean by help, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you felt sad or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know. I have days where I get quiet, sure, but “depressed” is a big word. Sometimes the weather does it, especially when it’s gray out, and then I’ll sit by the window and think about whether I watered the plants, and one thing leads to another.\n\nI guess I haven’t really been keeping track of it like that. Most days? I’m not sure I’d say that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t think so, no. It’s sort of been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago.\n\nThere might’ve been a good week here or there, like when I got some things sorted or the weather was nicer, but not a whole two months where I felt like how I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or low most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most days, exactly. Low is a funny word… like when the batteries are low in the remote, and then it only works if you hold it a certain way. I’ve had a lot of days where I’m just sort of… not up to much, but I don’t know if that’s “down.”\n\nSome days are all right if I keep busy with little things, though then I get sidetracked and the morning’s gone. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really counted the days like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I sit down to watch a program and then I realize I’m thinking about whether I left the laundry in, or that I need to wipe the counter, and then the program’s over. But that might just be normal, you know, getting older.\n\nI do lose my place sometimes if I’m reading the mail, especially those insurance papers with all the little print. My neighbor says nobody can make sense of those anyway. So… trouble concentrating? Maybe a little here and there, but I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure I’d use that word. It sounds very final, doesn’t it? Like when a plant is too far gone and you throw it out, though sometimes if you put it by the kitchen window it comes back a little.\n\nI do get discouraged, I suppose. But “hopeless,” I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble managing your bills?\n\nPatient: The bills… well, I try to keep them in the little basket by the phone, but then the church bulletin gets mixed in, and those envelopes all look the same, don’t they?\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s only bills, exactly. I’ve had more trouble keeping up with things I said I’d do. I used to help lead the choir and do little projects at the senior center, and lately I had to step back some because I don’t always follow through like I promised. My church friends check on me more now, which is kind of them, but it makes me feel… I don’t know, a little embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you they’ve noticed your memory or thinking has gotten worse?\n\nPatient: Other people… I don’t know if they’ve said it like that, exactly. My neighbor will sometimes say, “Now don’t forget,” but she says that to everybody, even when she’s reminding herself about the grocery coupons.\n\nMy church friends notice if I don’t bring something I said I would, but that could be because I put it by the door and then see a dish in the sink and—well, one thing leads to another. I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said my thinking is worse. I don’t keep track of days like that, you know. I just… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory or thinking seems worse than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Close to me… well, my sister will say, “You already told me that,” but she’s always been quick with me, even when we were girls sharing a room and she’d fuss over which drawer was hers.\n\nI don’t know that anyone has really told me my thinking is worse. They might remind me of things, church people especially, but nobody’s come right out and said it like that. I’ve always been careful, my mother used to say a dollar had corners so it wouldn’t roll away.\n\nBut I have stepped back from some things I used to keep up with better—like leading choir, and helping with those little senior-center projects—because I don’t always remember what I promised people I’d do. Then folks from church, they check on me more than they used to. Not exactly managing my money, maybe, but they do look in on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help keeping track of appointments?\n\nPatient: Appointments… I try to write them down when they give me the little card, though those cards are so small, and I’ll put one in my purse and then later I’m looking for a receipt from the grocery and there it is.\n\nI don’t know if I need help exactly. Sometimes someone will remind me, but I still try to keep track. It depends what you mean by help, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you felt sad or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know. I have days where I get quiet, sure, but “depressed” is a big word. Sometimes the weather does it, especially when it’s gray out, and then I’ll sit by the window and think about whether I watered the plants, and one thing leads to another.\n\nI guess I haven’t really been keeping track of it like that. Most days? I’m not sure I’d say that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less able to handle your usual day-to-day tasks than you were a couple of years ago?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in some ways. I used to be the one leading choir, you know, making sure everybody knew when to come in, and who had the song sheets. We had this one hymn—well, never mind.\n\nBut lately I had to step back from that, and from helping with those senior-center projects, because I don’t always keep up with what I said I’d do. I’ll promise somebody I’ll bring something or call someone, and then it slips away from me. So now some of the church folks check on me more than they used to. Not every little thing, but more than before."

Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.
